# Finite Time Analysis of Linear Two-timescale Stochastic Approximation with Markovian Noise

# Abstract

Linear two-timescale stochastic approximation (SA) scheme is an important class of algorithms which has become popular in reinforcement learning (RL), particularly for the policy evaluation problem. Recently, a number of works have been devoted to establishing the finite time analysis of the scheme, especially under the Markovian (non-i.i.d.) noise settings that are ubiquitous in practice. In this paper, we provide a finite-time analysis for linear two timescale SA. Our bounds show that there is no discrepancy in the convergence rate between Markovian and martingale noise, only the constants are affected by the mixing time of the Markov chain. With an appropriate step size schedule, the transient term in the expected error bound is  $o(1 / k^c)$  and the steady-state term is  $\mathcal{O}(1 / k)$ , where  $c > 1$  and  $k$  is the iteration number. Furthermore, we present an asymptotic expansion of the expected error with a matching lower bound of  $\Omega(1 / k)$ . A simple numerical experiment is presented to support our theory.

Keywords: stochastic approximation, reinforcement learning, GTD learning, Markovian noise

# 1. Introduction

Since its introduction close to 70 years ago, the stochastic approximation (SA) scheme (Robbins and Monro, 1951) has been a powerful tool for root finding when only noisy samples are available. During the past two decades, considerable progresses in the practical and theoretical research of SA have been made, see (Benaim, 1999; Kushner and Yin, 2003; Borkar, 2008) for an overview. Among others, linear SA schemes are popular in reinforcement learning (RL) as they lead to policy evaluation methods with linear function approximation, of particular importance is temporal difference (TD) learning (Sutton, 1988) for which finite time analysis has been reported in (Srikant and Ying, 2019; Lakshminarayanan and Szepesvari, 2018; Bhandari et al., 2018; Dalal et al., 2018a).

The TD learning scheme based on classical (linear) SA is known to be inadequate for the off-policy learning paradigms in RL, where data samples are drawn from a behavior policy different from the policy being evaluated (Baird, 1995; Tsitsiklis and Van Roy, 1997). To circumvent this

problem, Sutton et al. (2009a,b) have suggested to replace TD learning with the gradient TD (GTD) method or the TD with gradient correction (TDC) method. These methods fall within the scope of linear two-timescale SA scheme introduced by Borkar (1997):

$$
\theta_ {k + 1} = \theta_ {k} + \beta_ {k} \left\{\widetilde {b} _ {1} \left(X _ {k + 1}\right) - \widetilde {A} _ {1 1} \left(X _ {k + 1}\right) \theta_ {k} - \widetilde {A} _ {1 2} \left(X _ {k + 1}\right) w _ {k} \right\}, \tag {1}
$$

$$
w _ {k + 1} = w _ {k} + \gamma_ {k} \left\{\widetilde {b} _ {2} \left(X _ {k + 1}\right) - \widetilde {A} _ {2 1} \left(X _ {k + 1}\right) \theta_ {k} - \widetilde {A} _ {2 2} \left(X _ {k + 1}\right) w _ {k} \right\}. \tag {2}
$$

The above recursion involves two iterates,  $\theta_{k} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}}$ ,  $w_{k} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_{w}}$ , whose updates are coupled with each other. In the above,  $\widetilde{b}_{i}(x)$ ,  $\widetilde{A}_{ij}(x)$  are measurable vector/matrix valued functions on  $X$  and the random sequence  $(X_{k})_{k \geq 0}$ ,  $X_{k} \in X$  forms an ergodic Markov chain. The scalars  $\gamma_{k}, \beta_{k} > 0$  are step sizes. The above SA scheme is said to have two timescales as the step sizes satisfy  $\lim_{k \to \infty} \beta_{k} / \gamma_{k} < 1$  such that  $w_{k}$  is updated at a faster timescale. In fact,  $w_{k}$  is a 'tracking' term which seeks solution to a linear system characterized by  $\theta_{k}$ .

The goal of this paper is to characterize the finite time expected error bound with improved convergence rate for the two timescale SA (1),(2). The almost sure convergence of two timescale SA have been established in (Borkar, 1997; Tadic, 2004, 2006; Borkar, 2008), among others; the asymptotic convergence rates have been characterized in (Konda and Tsitsiklis, 2004; Mokkadem et al., 2006). However, finite-time risk bounds for two timescale SA have not been analyzed until recently. With martingale samples, Liu et al. (2015) provided the first finite time analysis of GTD method, Dalal et al. (2018b, 2019) provided improved finite time error bounds. Unlike our analysis, they analyzed modified two timescale SA with projection and their bounds hold with high probability. With Markovian noise, Gupta et al. (2019) studied the finite time expected error bound with constant step sizes; Xu et al. (2019) and Doan (2019) provided similar analysis for general step sizes. It is important to notice that with homogeneous martingale noise, the asymptotic rate of (1), (2) without a projection step, as shown in (Konda and Tsitsiklis, 2004, Theorem 2.6), is in the order  $\mathbb{E}[\| \theta_k - \theta^\star \| ^2 ] = \mathcal{O}(\beta_k),\mathbb{E}[\| w_k - A_{22}^{-1}(b_2 - A_{21}\theta_k)\| ^2 ] = \mathcal{O}(\gamma_k)$ , where  $\theta^{\star}$  is a stationary point of the SA scheme. However, the latter rate is not achieved in the finite-time error bounds analyzed by the above works except for (Dalal et al., 2019). It remains an open problem whether this error bound holds for the Markovian noise setting and for linear two time-scale SA scheme without projection.

# Contributions This paper has the following contributions:

- Improved Convergence Rate - We perform finite-time expected error bound analysis of the linear two timescale SA in both martingale and Markovian noise settings, in Theorems 1 & 2. Our analysis allows for general step sizes schedules [cf. A2, B4], including constant, piecewise constant, and diminishing step sizes explored in the prior works (Gupta et al., 2019; Dalal et al., 2019; Xu et al., 2019; Doan, 2019). We show that the error bound consists of a transient and a steady-state term, and the asymptotic rate is obtained from the latter. We show that this asymptotic rate matches those in (Konda and Tsitsiklis, 2004, Theorem 2.6), i.e.,  $\mathbb{E}[\| \theta_k - \theta^\star \|^2] = \mathcal{O}(\beta_k)$ ,  $\mathbb{E}[\| w_k - A_{22}^{-1}(b_2 - A_{21}\theta_k)\|^2] = \mathcal{O}(\gamma_k)$ . In particular, the fastest achievable rate for  $\mathbb{E}[\| \theta_k - \theta^\star \|^2]$  will be  $\mathcal{O}(1 / k)$  when we set  $\beta_k = \mathcal{O}(1 / k)$ ,  $\gamma_k = \mathcal{O}(1 / k^v)$  with  $v < 1$ .  
- Novel Analysis without A-prori Stability Assumption – Unlike the prior works (Liu et al., 2015; Dalal et al., 2019; Xu et al., 2019), our convergence results are obtained without requiring a projection step throughout the SA iterations. In fact, Dalal et al. (2019) have pointed out that the projection step is merely included to ensure a-prori stability of the algorithm, and is often not used in practice. Our relaxation and the ability to achieve the optimal convergence rate are

obtained through a tight analysis of the recursive inequalities of the (cross-)variances of  $\theta_{k}$ ,  $w_{k}$  see Section 3.

- Asymptotic Expansion - With an additional assumption on the step size, we compute an exact asymptotic expansion of the expected error  $\mathbb{E}[\| \theta_k - \theta^\star \| ^2 ]$ , see Theorem 10. With an appropriate diminishing step sizes schedule, we show that the expected error cannot be smaller than  $\Omega (\beta_{k})$  which matches our upper bound results in Theorem 1 & 2.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we present the detailed conditions for two timescale linear SA, and the main results on finite-time performance bounds. In Section 3, we provide an outline of the proof, illustrating the insights behind the main steps. In Section 4, we show that the finite-time error bounds are tight by quantifying an exact expansion of the covariance of iterates. In Section 5, we illustrate the theoretical findings using numerical experiments.

Notations Let  $n \in \mathbb{N}$  and  $Q$  be a symmetric definite  $n \times n$  matrix. For  $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$ , we denote  $\|x\|_Q = \{x^\top Qx\}^{1/2}$ . For brevity, we set  $\|x\| = \|x\|_1$ . Let  $m \in \mathbb{N}$ ,  $P$  be a symmetric definite  $m \times m$  matrix,  $A$  be an  $n \times m$  matrix. A matrix  $A$  is said to be Hurwitz if the real parts of its eigenvalues are strictly negative. We denote  $\|A\|_{P,Q} = \max_{\|x\|_P = 1} \|Ax\|_Q$ . If  $A$  is a  $n \times n$  matrix, we denote  $\|A\|_Q = \|A\|_{Q,Q}$ . Lastly, we give a number of auxiliary lemmas in Appendix D that are instrumental to our analysis.

# 2. Linear Two Time-scale Stochastic Approximation (SA) Scheme

We investigate the linear two timescale SA given by the following equivalent form of (1), (2):

$$
\theta_ {k + 1} = \theta_ {k} + \beta_ {k} \left(b _ {1} - A _ {1 1} \theta_ {k} - A _ {1 2} w _ {k} + V _ {k + 1}\right), \tag {3}
$$

$$
w _ {k + 1} = w _ {k} + \gamma_ {k} \left(b _ {2} - A _ {2 1} \theta_ {k} - A _ {2 2} w _ {k} + W _ {k + 1}\right), \tag {4}
$$

where the mean fields are defined as  $b_{i} \coloneqq \lim_{k\to \infty}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{b}_{i}(X_{k})]$ ,  $A_{ij} \coloneqq \lim_{k\to \infty}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{A}_{ij}(X_k)]$  (these limits exist as we recall that  $(X_{k})_{k\geq 0}$  is an ergodic Markov chain). The noise terms  $V_{k + 1}, W_{k + 1}$  are given by:

$$
V _ {k + 1} := \widetilde {b} _ {1} (X _ {k + 1}) - b _ {1} - (\widetilde {A} _ {1 1} (X _ {k + 1}) - A _ {1 1}) \theta_ {k} - (\widetilde {A} _ {1 2} (X _ {k + 1}) - A _ {1 2}) w _ {k},
$$

$$
W _ {k + 1} := \widetilde {b} _ {2} \left(X _ {k + 1}\right) - b _ {2} - \left(\widetilde {A} _ {2 1} \left(X _ {k + 1}\right) - A _ {2 1}\right) \theta_ {k} - \left(\widetilde {A} _ {2 2} \left(X _ {k + 1}\right) - A _ {2 2}\right) w _ {k}. \tag {5}
$$

The goal of the recursion (3), (4) is to find a stationary solution pair  $(\theta^{\star}, w^{\star})$  that solves the system of linear equations:

$$
A _ {1 1} \theta + A _ {1 2} w = b _ {1}, \quad A _ {2 1} \theta + A _ {2 2} w = b _ {2}. \tag {6}
$$

We are interested in the scenario when the solution pair  $(\theta^{\star},w^{\star})$  is unique and is given by

$$
\theta^ {\star} = \Delta^ {- 1} \left(b _ {1} - A _ {1 2} A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} b _ {2}\right), \quad w ^ {\star} = A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} \left(b _ {2} - A _ {2 1} \theta^ {\star}\right). \tag {7}
$$

where  $\Delta := A_{11} - A_{12}A_{22}^{-1}A_{21}$ . To analyze the convergence of  $(\theta_k, w_k)_{k \geq 0}$  in (3), (4) to  $(\theta^\star, w^\star)$ , we require the following assumptions:

A1 Matrices  $-A_{22}$  and  $-\Delta = -(A_{11} - A_{12}A_{22}^{-1}A_{21})$  are Hurwitz.

The above assumption is common for linear two time-scale SA, see (Konda and Tsitsiklis, 2004). As a consequence, using the Lyapunov lemma (stated in Lemma 16 in the appendix for completeness), there exist positive definite matrices  $Q_{22}^{\top} = Q_{22} \succ 0$ ,  $Q_{\Delta}^{\top} = Q_{\Delta} \succ 0$  satisfying

$$
A _ {2 2} ^ {\top} Q _ {2 2} + Q _ {2 2} A _ {2 2} = \mathrm {I}, \quad Q _ {\Delta} \Delta + \Delta^ {\top} Q _ {\Delta} = \mathrm {I}. \tag {8}
$$

This ensures the contraction (see Lemma 17 in the appendix):

$$
\left\| \mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 2} \right\| _ {Q _ {2 2}} \leq 1 - a _ {2 2} \gamma_ {k}, \quad \left\| \mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} \Delta \right\| _ {Q _ {\Delta}} \leq 1 - a _ {\Delta} \beta_ {k}, \tag {9}
$$

where  $a_{22} \coloneqq 1 / (2\| Q_{22}\|^2)$ ,  $a_{\Delta} \coloneqq 1 / (2\| Q_{\Delta}\|^2)$ . We consider the following conditions on the step sizes:

A2  $(\gamma_k)_{k\geq 0}$ ,  $(\beta_k)_{k\geq 0}$  are nonincreasing sequences of positive numbers that satisfy the following.

1. There exist constants  $\kappa$  such that for all  $k\in \mathbb{N}$ , we have  $\beta_{k} / \gamma_{k}\leq \kappa$

2. For all  $k\in \mathbb{N}$  , it holds

$$
\gamma_ {k} / \gamma_ {k + 1} \leq 1 + (a _ {2 2} / 8) \gamma_ {k + 1}, \beta_ {k} / \beta_ {k + 1} \leq 1 + (a _ {\Delta} / 1 6) \beta_ {k + 1}, \gamma_ {k} / \gamma_ {k + 1} \leq 1 + (a _ {\Delta} / 1 6) \beta_ {k + 1}.
$$

As a consequence, we can define  $\varsigma := 1 + \{\gamma_0a_{22} / 8 \vee \beta_0a_\Delta / 16\}$  such that  $\gamma_k / \gamma_{k+1} \leq \varsigma$ ,  $\beta_k / \beta_{k+1} \leq \varsigma$ . Our conditions on step sizes are similar to (Konda and Tsitsiklis, 2004, Assumption 2.3, 2.5). These conditions encompass diminishing, piecewise constant and constant step sizes schedules which are common in the literature. For instance, a popular choice of diminishing step sizes satisfying A2 is

$$
\beta_ {k} = c ^ {\beta} / \left(k + k _ {0} ^ {\beta}\right), \quad \gamma_ {k} = c ^ {\gamma} / \left(k + k _ {0} ^ {\gamma}\right) ^ {2 / 3} \tag {10}
$$

with some constants  $c^{\beta}$ ,  $c^{\gamma}$ ,  $k_0^{\gamma}$ ,  $k_0^{\beta}$ , e.g., as suggested in (Dalal et al., 2018b, Remark 9); or a constant step size of  $\beta_{k} = \beta$ ,  $\gamma_{k} = \gamma$ ; or a piecewise constant step size, e.g., (Gupta et al., 2019).

We present new results on the convergence rate of (3), (4) depending on the types of noise with  $V_{k + 1}, W_{k + 1}$ . To discuss these cases, let us define the  $\sigma$ -field generated by the two timescale SA scheme and the initial error made by the SA scheme, respectively as:

$$
\mathcal {F} _ {k} := \sigma \left\{\theta_ {0}, w _ {0}, X _ {1}, X _ {2}, \dots , X _ {k} \right\}, \quad \mathrm {V} _ {0} := \mathbb {E} \left[ \| \theta^ {0} - \theta^ {\star} \| ^ {2} + \| w ^ {0} - w ^ {\star} \| ^ {2} \right]. \tag {11}
$$

Our main results are presented as follows.

Martingale Noise We consider a simple setting where the random elements  $X_{k}$  are drawn i.i.d. from the stationary distribution such that  $b_{i},A_{ij}$  are the expected values of  $\widetilde{b}_i(X_k),\widetilde{A}_{ij}(X_k)$ . Furthermore, the random variables  $\widetilde{b}_i(X_k),\widetilde{A}_{ij}(X_k)$  have bounded second order moment. Note that this implies  $\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{F}_k}[V_{k + 1}] = \mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{F}_k}[W_{k + 1}] = 0$ , i.e., the sequences  $(V_{k + 1})_{k\in \mathbb{N}},(W_{k + 1})_{k\in \mathbb{N}}$  are martingale difference sequences. Formally, we describe this setting as the following conditions on  $V_{k + 1},W_{k + 1}$ :

A3 The noise terms are zero-mean conditioned on  $\mathcal{F}_k$ , i.e.,  $\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{F}_k}[V_{k + 1}] = \mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{F}_k}[W_{k + 1}] = 0$  
A4 There exist constants  $m_W, m_V$  such that

$$
\left\| \mathbb {E} \left[ V _ {k + 1} V _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} \right] \right\| \leq m _ {V} \left(1 + \left\| \mathbb {E} \left[ \theta_ {k} \theta_ {k} ^ {\top} \right] \right\| + \left\| \mathbb {E} \left[ w _ {k} w _ {k} ^ {\top} \right] \right\|\right),
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbb {E} \left[ W _ {k + 1} W _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} \right] \right\| \leq m _ {W} \left(1 + \left\| \mathbb {E} \left[ \theta_ {k} \theta_ {k} ^ {\top} \right] \right\| + \left\| \mathbb {E} \left[ w _ {k} w _ {k} ^ {\top} \right] \right\|\right).
$$

Theorem 1 Assume  $A1-4$  and for all  $k \in \mathbb{N}$ , we have  $\gamma_k \in [0, \gamma_\infty^{\mathrm{mtg}}]$ ,  $\beta_k \in [0, \beta_\infty^{\mathrm{mtg}}]$  and  $\kappa \in [0, \kappa_\infty]$ , where  $\gamma_\infty^{\mathrm{mtg}}, \beta_\infty^{\mathrm{mtg}}, \kappa_\infty$  are constants defined in (20), (16). Then

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \| \theta_ {k} - \theta^ {*} \| ^ {2} \right] \leq d _ {\theta} \left\{\mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \mathrm {m t g}} \prod_ {\ell = 0} ^ {k - 1} \left(1 - \beta_ {\ell} \frac {a _ {\Delta}}{4}\right) \mathrm {V} _ {0} + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \mathrm {m t g}} \beta_ {k} \right\} \tag {12}
$$

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \| w _ {k} - A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} \left(b _ {2} - A _ {2 1} \theta_ {k}\right) \| ^ {2} \right] \leq d _ {w} \left\{\mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\widehat {w}, \mathrm {m t g}} \prod_ {\ell = 0} ^ {k - 1} \left(1 - \beta_ {\ell} \frac {a _ {\Delta}}{4}\right) \mathrm {V} _ {0} + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\widehat {w}, \mathrm {m t g}} \gamma_ {k} \right\} \tag {13}
$$

The exact constants are provided in the appendix, see (55), (58).

Markovian Noise Consider the sequence  $(X_{k})_{k\geq 0}$  to be samples from an exogenous Markov chain on  $X$  with the transition kernel  $\mathrm{P}:\mathsf{X}\times \mathsf{X}\to \mathbb{R}_{+}$ . For any measurable function  $f$ , we have

$$
\mathbb {E} ^ {\mathcal {F} _ {k}} \left[ f (X _ {k + 1}) \right] = \mathrm {P} f (X _ {k}) = \int_ {\mathsf {X}} f (x) \mathrm {P} (X _ {k}, \mathrm {d} x)
$$

We state the following assumptions:

B1 The Markov kernel  $\mathrm{P}$  has a unique invariant distribution  $\mu : \mathsf{X} \to \mathbb{R}_{+}$ . Moreover, it is irreducible and aperiodic.

Observe that

$$
b _ {i} = \int_ {\mathsf {X}} \widetilde {b} _ {i} (x) \mu (\mathrm {d} x), A _ {i j} = \int_ {\mathsf {X}} \widetilde {A} _ {i j} (x) \mu (\mathrm {d} x), i, j = 1, 2.
$$

We show that the linear two time-scale SA (1), (2) converges to a unique fixed point defined by the above mean field vectors/matrices, see (7). An important condition that enables our analysis is the existence of a solution to the following Poisson equation:

B2 For any  $i, j = 1, 2$ , consider  $\widetilde{b}_i(x)$ ,  $\widetilde{A}_{ij}(x)$ , there exists vector/matrix valued measurable functions  $\widehat{b}_i(x)$ ,  $\widehat{A}_{ij}(x)$  which satisfy

$$
\widetilde {b} _ {i} (x) - b _ {i} = \widehat {b} _ {i} (x) - \mathrm {P} \widehat {b} _ {i} (x), \widetilde {A} _ {i j} (x) - A _ {i j} = \widehat {A} _ {i j} (x) - \mathrm {P} \widehat {A} _ {i j} (x)
$$

for any  $x \in \mathsf{X}$  and  $b_i, A_{ij}$  are the mean fields of  $\widetilde{b}_i(x), \widetilde{A}_{ij}(x)$  with the stationary distribution  $\mu$ .

The above assumption can be guaranteed under B1 together with some regularity conditions, see (Douc et al., 2018, Section 21.2). Moreover,

B3 Under B2, the vector/matrix valued functions  $\widehat{b}_i(x),\widehat{A}_{ij}(x)$  are uniformly bounded: for any  $i,j = 1,2,x\in \mathsf{X}$

$$
\| \widehat {b} _ {i} (x) \| \leq \overline {{\mathrm {b}}}, \| \widehat {A} _ {i j} (x) \| \leq \overline {{\mathrm {A}}}.
$$

B4 There exists constant  $\rho_0$  such that for any  $k\geq 1$ , we have  $\gamma_{k - 1}^2\leq \rho_0\beta_k$ .

To satisfy B3, we observe that the bounds  $\overline{\mathsf{b}},\overline{\mathsf{A}}$  depend on the mixing time of the chain  $(X_{k})_{k\geq 0}$  and a uniform bound on  $\widetilde{b}_i(\cdot),\widetilde{A}_{ij}(\cdot)$ . In the context of reinforcement learning, the latter can be satisfied when the feature vectors and reward are bounded. Note that B3 implies A4, see Section 3.2. Meanwhile, B4 imposes further restriction on the step size. The latter can also be satisfied by (10).

The challenges of analysis with Markovian noise lie in the biasedness of the noise term as  $\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{F}_k}[V_{k + 1}] \neq 0$ ,  $\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{F}_k}[W_{k + 1}] \neq 0$ . With a careful analysis, we obtain:

Theorem 2 Assume A1-2, B1-4 hold and for all  $k \in \mathbb{N}$ , we have  $\beta_k \in (0, \beta_{\infty}^{\mathrm{mark}}]$ ,  $\gamma_k \in (0, \gamma_{\infty}^{\mathrm{mark}}]$ ,  $\kappa \leq \kappa_{\infty}$ , where  $\beta_{\infty}^{\mathrm{mark}}$ ,  $\gamma_{\infty}^{\mathrm{mark}}$ ,  $\kappa_{\infty}$  are defined in (27), (16). Then

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \| \theta_ {k} - \theta^ {\star} \| ^ {2} \right] \leq d _ {\theta} \left\{\mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \text {m a r k}} \prod_ {\ell = 0} ^ {k - 1} \left(1 - \beta_ {\ell} \frac {a _ {\Delta}}{8}\right) \left(1 + \mathrm {V} _ {0}\right) + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \text {m a r k}} \beta_ {k} \right\}, \tag {14}
$$

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \| w _ {k} - A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} \left(b _ {2} - A _ {2 1} \theta_ {k}\right) \| ^ {2} \right] \leq d _ {w} \left\{\mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\widehat {w}, \text {m a r k}} \prod_ {\ell = 0} ^ {k - 1} \left(1 - \beta_ {\ell} \frac {a _ {\Delta}}{8}\right) \left(1 + \mathrm {V} _ {0}\right) + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\widehat {w}, \text {m a r k}} \gamma_ {k} \right\}. \tag {15}
$$

The exact constants are given in the appendix, see (78), (80).

While Theorem 2 relaxes the martingale difference assumption A4 in Theorem 1, we remark that the results here do not generalize that in Theorem 1 due to the additional B3, B4. Particularly, with martingale noise, the convergence of linear two timescale SA only requires the noise to have bounded second order moment, yet the Markovian noise needs to be uniformly bounded.

Convergence Rate of Linear Two Timescale SA The upper bounds in Theorem 1 and 2 consist of two terms - the first term is a 'transient' error with product such as  $\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}(1-\beta_i a_\Delta/8)$  decays to zero at the rate  $o(1/k^c)$  for some  $c > 1$  under an appropriate choice of step sizes such as (10); the second term is a 'steady-state' error. We observe that the 'steady-state' error of the iterates  $\theta_k, w_k$  exhibit different behaviors. Taking the step size choices in (10) as an example, the steady-state error of the slow-update iterates  $\theta_k$  is  $\mathcal{O}(1/k)$  while the error of fast-update iterates  $w_k$  is  $\mathcal{O}(1/k^{\frac{2}{3}})$ . Furthermore, similar bounds hold for both martingale and Markovian noise. In Section 4 we show that the obtained rates are also tight.

Comparison to Related Works Our results improve the convergence rate analysis of linear two timescale SA in a number of recent works. In the martingale noise setting (Theorem 1), the closest work to ours is (Dalal et al., 2019) which analyzed the linear two timescale SA with martingale samples and diminishing step sizes. The authors improved on (Dalal et al., 2018b) and obtained the same convergence rate (in high probability) as our Theorem 1, furthermore it is demonstrated that the obtained rates are tight. Their bounds also exhibit a sublinear dependence on the dimensions  $d_{\theta}, d_{w}$ . However, their algorithm involves a sparsely executed projection step and the error bound holds only for a sufficiently large  $k$ . These restrictions are lifted in our analysis.

In the Markovian noise setting (Theorem 2), the closest works to ours are (Doan, 2019; Gupta et al., 2019; Xu et al., 2019). In particular, Gupta et al. (2019) analyzed the linear two timescale SA with constant step sizes and showed that the steady-state error for both  $\theta_{k}, w_{k}$  is  $\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{2} / \beta)$ . Xu et al. (2019) analyzed the TDC algorithm with a projection step and showed that the steady-state error for  $\theta_{k}$  is  $\mathcal{O}(1 / k^{\frac{2}{3}})$  if the step sizes in (10) is used. Doan (2019) analyzed the linear two timescale SA with diminishing step size and showed that the steady state error for both  $\theta_{k}, w_{k}$  is  $\mathcal{O}(1 / k^{\frac{2}{3}})$ . Interestingly, the above works do not obtain the fast rate in Theorem 2, i.e.,  $\mathbb{E}[\| \theta_k - \theta^\star \| ^2 ] = \mathcal{O}(1 / k)$ . One of the reasons for the sub-optimality in their rates is that their analysis are based on building a single Lyapunov function that controls both errors in  $\theta_{k}$  and  $w_{k}$ . In contrast, our analysis relies on a set of coupled inequalities to obtain tight bounds for each of the iterates  $\theta_{k}, w_{k}$ .

# 3. Convergence Analysis

While much of the technical details and the complete constants of non-asymptotic bounds will be postponed to the appendix, this section offers insights into our main theoretical results through

sketching the major steps involved in proving Theorem 1 & 2. Throughout, we shall consider the following bounds on the step sizes and step size ratio:

$$
\beta_ {\infty} ^ {(0)} := \frac {1}{2 \| Q _ {\Delta} \| ^ {2} \| \Delta \| _ {Q _ {\Delta}} ^ {2}} \wedge \frac {1}{2 \| \Delta \| _ {Q _ {\Delta}} + a _ {\Delta}}, \gamma_ {\infty} ^ {(0)} := \frac {1}{2 \| Q _ {2 2} \| ^ {2} \| A _ {2 2} \| _ {Q _ {2 2}} ^ {2}},
$$

$$
\kappa_ {\infty} := \left(\frac {a _ {2 2} / 2}{\| A _ {1 2} \| _ {Q _ {2 2} , Q _ {\Delta}} \| A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} A _ {2 1} \| _ {Q _ {\Delta} , Q _ {2 2}} + \frac {a _ {\Delta}}{2}} \left\{1 \wedge \frac {a _ {\Delta} / 2}{\| \Delta \| _ {Q _ {\Delta}} + \frac {a _ {\Delta}}{2}} \right\}\right) \wedge \frac {a _ {2 2}}{4 a _ {\Delta}}. \tag {16}
$$

To begin with, let us present the reformulation of the two time-scale SA scheme (3), (4) that is borrowed from (Konda and Tsitsiklis, 2004). Define:

$$
L _ {k + 1} := \big (L _ {k} - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 2} L _ {k} + \beta_ {k} A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} A _ {2 1} (\Delta - A _ {1 2} L _ {k}) \big) \big (\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} (\Delta - A _ {1 2} L _ {k}) \big) ^ {- 1}, \quad L _ {0} := 0,
$$

and  $\mathrm{L}_{\infty} := a_{\Delta} / (2\|A_{12}\|_{Q_{22}, Q_{\Delta}})$ . As shown in Lemma 18 of the appendix, with the step sizes  $\gamma_k \leq \gamma_{\infty}^{(0)}$ ,  $\beta_k \leq \beta_{\infty}^{(0)}$ ,  $\kappa \leq \kappa_{\infty}$ , the above recursion on  $L_k$  is well defined where it holds that  $\|L_k\|_{Q_{\Delta}, Q_{22}} \leq \mathrm{L}_{\infty}$  for any  $k \geq 0$ . In addition, define the matrices:

$$
B _ {1 1} ^ {k} := \Delta - A _ {1 2} L _ {k}, \quad B _ {2 2} ^ {k} := \frac {\beta_ {k}}{\gamma_ {k}} \big (L _ {k + 1} + A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} A _ {2 1} \big) A _ {1 2} + A _ {2 2}, \quad C _ {k} := L _ {k + 1} + A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} A _ {2 1}.
$$

We obtain a simplified two timescale SA recursions (proof in Appendix A):

Observation 1 Consider the following change-of-variables:

$$
\tilde {\theta} _ {k} := \theta_ {k} - \theta^ {\star}, \quad \tilde {w} _ {k} = w _ {k} - w ^ {\star} + C _ {k - 1} \tilde {\theta} _ {k}. \tag {17}
$$

The two time-scale SA (3), (4) is equivalent to the following iterations:

$$
\tilde {\theta} _ {k + 1} = \left(\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k}\right) \tilde {\theta} _ {k} - \beta_ {k} A _ {1 2} \tilde {w} _ {k} - \beta_ {k} V _ {k + 1}. \tag {18}
$$

$$
\tilde {w} _ {k + 1} = \left(\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} B _ {2 2} ^ {k}\right) \tilde {w} _ {k} - \beta_ {k} C _ {k} V _ {k + 1} - \gamma_ {k} W _ {k + 1}. \tag {19}
$$

Observe that  $\tilde{\theta}_k = 0$ ,  $\tilde{w}_k = 0$  is equivalent to having  $\theta_k = \theta^\star$ ,  $w_k = w^\star$ , i.e., the two timescale SA solves the linear system of equations (6). The simplified recursion (18), (19) decouples the update of  $\tilde{w}_k$  from  $\tilde{\theta}_k$ . This allows one to treat the  $\tilde{w}_k$  update as a one timescale linear SA, and therefore provides a shortcut to perform a tight analysis. We focus on estimating the following operator norms of covariances:

$$
\mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {w}} := | | \mathbb {E} [ \tilde {w} _ {k} \tilde {w} _ {k} ^ {\top} ] | |, \quad \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} := | | \mathbb {E} [ \tilde {\theta} _ {k} \tilde {\theta} _ {k} ^ {\top} ] | |, \quad \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} := | | \mathbb {E} [ \tilde {\theta} _ {k} \tilde {w} _ {k} ^ {\top} ] | |,
$$

which are respectively the covariance for  $w_{k}$ ,  $\theta_{k}$  and the cross-variance between  $w_{k}$ ,  $\theta_{k}$ .

# 3.1. Proof Outline of Theorem 1

For this theorem, we assume the step sizes and their ratio are chosen such that

$$
\gamma_ {k} \leq \gamma_ {\infty} ^ {\mathrm {m t g}} := \gamma_ {\infty} ^ {(0)} \wedge \frac {1}{\frac {a _ {2 2}}{2} + \frac {2}{a _ {2 2}} p _ {2 2} \left(\tilde {m} _ {V} + \kappa^ {2} \tilde {m} _ {W}\right)} \wedge \frac {a _ {\Delta}}{4 \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {\theta}}}, \beta_ {k} \leq \beta_ {\infty} ^ {\mathrm {m t g}} := \beta_ {\infty} ^ {(0)}, \tag {20}
$$

where  $p_{22} = \lambda_{\min}^{-1}(Q_{22})\lambda_{\max}(Q_{22})$  and  $\mathrm{C}_2^{\hat{\theta}}$  is defined in (55) in the appendix.

While the property which the noise terms satisfy  $\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{F}_k}[V_{k + 1}] = 0$ ,  $\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{F}_k}[W_{k + 1}] = 0$  has greatly simplified the analysis, the challenge with our analysis lies in the coupling between slow and fast updating iterates whose convergence rates must be carefully characterized in order to obtain the desired rate in Theorem 1. To summarize, our proof consists of three steps in order: (i) we bound  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{w}}$  with an inequality that is coupled with  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta}}$ ; then (ii) we bound the cross term  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{w}}$  using an inequality coupled with  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta}}$ ; lastly, (iii) these bounds are combined to bound  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta}}$ .

Step 1: Bounding  $\mathbf{M}_k^{\tilde{w}}$  Upon applying the variable transformation in Observation 1, (19) can be treated as a one-timescale SA which updates  $\tilde{w}_k$  independently, and the contributions from  $\tilde{\theta}_k$  are only found in the noise term, as seen from (34). This leads to:

Proposition 3 Assume A1-4 and the step sizes satisfy (20). For any  $k\in \mathbb{N}$ , it holds

$$
\mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {w}} \leq \prod_ {\ell = 0} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} a _ {2 2}}{2}\right) \frac {\lambda_ {\max } (Q _ {2 2})}{\lambda_ {\min } (Q _ {2 2})} \mathrm {M} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w}} + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {w}} \gamma_ {k + 1} + \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {w}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} \prod_ {\ell = j + 1} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} a _ {2 2}}{2}\right) \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}}, \tag {21}
$$

where the constants  $\mathrm{C}_1^{\tilde{w}},\mathrm{C}_2^{\tilde{w}}$  can be found in (42) in the appendix.

The right hand side of (21) consists of three components: (i) a fast decaying term relying on the product  $\prod_{\ell=0}^{k}(1 - \gamma_{\ell}a_{22}/2)$ , (ii) an  $\mathcal{O}(\gamma_k)$  term, and (iii) a convolutive term between  $\mathrm{M}_k^\theta$  and the fast decaying term depending on the step size sequence  $(\gamma_k)_{k\geq 0}$ . In the above, the second term can be viewed as a 'steady-state' term.

Step 2: Bounding  $\mathbf{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{w}}$  Observe that  $\mathbf{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{w}}$  refers to the cross variance between  $\tilde{w}_k$  and  $\tilde{\theta}_k$ . We show that utilizing (18), (19), (21) allows us to derive:

Proposition 4 Assume A1-4 and the step sizes satisfy (20). For any  $k\in \mathbb{N}$ , it holds

$$
\mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} \leq \mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} \prod_ {\ell = 0} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} a _ {2 2}}{2}\right) + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} \beta_ {k + 1} + \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} \prod_ {\ell = j + 1} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} a _ {2 2}}{2}\right) \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}}, \tag {22}
$$

where the constants  $\mathrm{C}_0^{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{w}},\mathrm{C}_1^{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{w}},\mathrm{C}_2^{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{w}}$  can be found in (49) in the appendix.

The above bound is a crucial step in obtaining the  $\mathcal{O}(\beta_k)$  rate for  $\mathrm{M}_k^\theta$ . To better appreciate it, note that as  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{w}} \leq (\sqrt{d_\theta d_w}/2)\{\mathrm{M}_k^\tilde{\theta} + \mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{w}}\}$  (see Lemma 23 in the appendix), one can derive a similar result to (22) by merely applying Proposition 3. However, doing so results in an overestimated 'steady-state' error of  $\mathcal{O}(\gamma_k)$  which is worse than the  $\mathcal{O}(\beta_k)$  error in (22). On the other hand, we take care of the two timescale nature of the algorithm to obtain (22) with the fast rate.

Step 3: Bounding  $\mathbf{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta}}$  Having equipped ourselves with Proposition 3 and 4, we can analyze  $\mathbf{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta}}$  using (18) and the derived bounds on  $\mathbf{M}_k^{\tilde{w}},\mathbf{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{w}}$ , this leads to

Proposition 5 Assume A1-4 and the step sizes satisfy (20). For any  $k\in \mathbb{N}$ , it holds

$$
\mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \leq \mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \prod_ {\ell = 0} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\beta_ {\ell} a _ {\Delta}}{2}\right) + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \beta_ {k + 1} + \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} \beta_ {j} \prod_ {\ell = j + 1} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\beta_ {\ell} a _ {\Delta}}{2}\right) \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}}, \tag {23}
$$

where the constants  $\mathrm{C}_0^{\tilde{\theta}},\mathrm{C}_1^{\tilde{\theta}},\mathrm{C}_2^{\tilde{\theta}}$  are given in (55) in the appendix.

Besides that the middle term is now  $\mathcal{O}(\beta_k)$ , we also observe that the convolution term with  $(\mathrm{M}_j^\theta)_{j\geq 0}$  depends on the product of step sizes  $\beta_{j}\gamma_{j}$ . This bound is obtained using Proposition 4 and the fact that the cross variance  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{w}}$  has a steady-state error of  $\mathcal{O}(\beta_k)$ .

Eq. (23) is a recursive inequality as  $\mathrm{M}_k^\theta$  are found on both sides. In the appendix, we show that there exists a sequence  $(\mathrm{U}_k)_{k\geq 0}$  satisfying  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta}}\leq \mathrm{U}_k$  and

$$
\mathrm {U} _ {k + 1} \leq \left(1 - \beta_ {k} a _ {\Delta} / 4\right) \mathrm {U} _ {k} + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \left(a _ {\Delta} / 2\right) \beta_ {k} ^ {2} \tag {24}
$$

for some constant  $\mathrm{C}_1^\theta$ . This immediately leads to (12), followed by (13) similarly.

# 3.2. Proof Outline of Theorem 2

While our proof has largely followed the same strategy as in the martingale noise case, now that the main challenge in handling the Markovian noise case is that the noise terms  $V_{k + 1}, W_{k + 1}$  are no longer (conditionally) zero-mean. To circumvent this difficulty, we recall B2 and define the following using the solution of the Poisson equation: for any  $i, j = 1, 2$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \psi_ {k} ^ {b _ {i}} := \mathrm {P} \widehat {b} _ {i} (X _ {k}), \quad \Psi_ {k} ^ {A _ {i j}} := \mathrm {P} \widehat {A} _ {i j} (X _ {k}), \\ \xi_ {k} ^ {b _ {i}} := \widehat {b} _ {i} (X _ {k + 1}) - \mathrm {P} \widehat {b} _ {i} (X _ {k}), \quad \Xi_ {k} ^ {A _ {i j}} := \widehat {A} _ {i j} (X _ {k + 1}) - \mathrm {P} \widehat {A} _ {i j} (X _ {k}), \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\xi_k^{b_i}, \Xi_k^{A_{ij}}$  are zero mean when conditioned on  $\mathcal{F}_k$ . The noise terms (5) can be rewritten as

$$
\begin{array}{l} V _ {k + 1} = \underbrace {\xi_ {k} ^ {b _ {1}} + \Xi_ {k} ^ {A _ {1 1}} \theta_ {k} + \Xi_ {k} ^ {A _ {1 2}} w _ {k}} _ {=: V _ {k + 1} ^ {(0)}} + \underbrace {(\psi_ {k} ^ {b _ {1}} - \psi_ {k + 1} ^ {b _ {1}}) + (\Psi_ {k} ^ {A _ {1 1}} - \Psi_ {k + 1} ^ {A _ {1 1}}) \theta_ {k} + (\Psi_ {k} ^ {A _ {1 2}} - \Psi_ {k + 1} ^ {A _ {1 2}}) w _ {k}} _ {=: V _ {k + 1} ^ {(1)}} \\ W _ {k + 1} = \underbrace {\xi_ {k} ^ {b _ {2}} + \Xi_ {k} ^ {A _ {2 1}} \theta_ {k} + \Xi_ {k} ^ {A _ {2 2}} w _ {k}} _ {=: W _ {k + 1} ^ {(0)}} + \underbrace {\left(\psi_ {k} ^ {b _ {2}} - \psi_ {k + 1} ^ {b _ {2}}\right) + \left(\Psi_ {k} ^ {A _ {2 1}} - \Psi_ {k + 1} ^ {A _ {2 1}}\right) \theta_ {k} + \left(\Psi_ {k} ^ {A _ {2 2}} - \Psi_ {k + 1} ^ {A _ {2 2}}\right) w _ {k}} _ {=: W _ {k + 1} ^ {(1)}}. \tag {25} \\ \end{array}
$$

We observe that  $\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{F}_k}\left[V_{k + 1}^{(0)}\right] = 0, \mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{F}_k}\left[W_{k + 1}^{(0)}\right] = 0$  and therefore (25) separates the noise terms into their martingale  $(V_k^{(0)},W_k^{(0)})$  and Markovian  $(V_k^{(1)},W_k^{(1)})$  components. Under B3, the second order moment of these noise components satisfy A4. Accordingly, we define  $\tilde{\theta}_0^{(0)} = \tilde{\theta}_0,\tilde{\theta}_0^{(1)} = 0$  and  $\tilde{w}_0^{(0)} = \tilde{w}_0,\tilde{w}_0^{(1)} = 0$  and the recursions:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \tilde {\theta} _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)} = \left(\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k}\right) \tilde {\theta} _ {k} ^ {(i)} - \beta_ {k} A _ {1 2} \tilde {w} _ {k} ^ {(i)} - \beta_ {k} V _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)}, i = 0, 1, \\ \begin{array}{l} \tilde {w} _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)} = \left(\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {2 2} ^ {k}\right) \tilde {w} _ {k} ^ {(i)} - \gamma_ {k} \left(W _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)} + C _ {k} V _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)}\right), i = 0, 1, \end{array} \tag {26} \\ \end{array}
$$

where it holds that  $\tilde{\theta}_k = \tilde{\theta}_k^{(0)} + \tilde{\theta}_k^{(1)}$ ,  $\tilde{w}_k = \tilde{w}_k^{(0)} + \tilde{w}_k^{(1)}$ . Clearly,  $\tilde{\theta}_k^{(0)}, \tilde{w}_k^{(0)}$  (resp.  $\tilde{\theta}_k^{(1)}, \tilde{w}_k^{(1)}$ ) are iterates of the two timescale SA driven by martingale (resp. Markovian) noise. The two sets of recursions are independent except the second order moments of noise are bounded by  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta}}$ ,  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{w}}$ , containing the contributions from  $\tilde{\theta}_k^{(0)}$ ,  $\tilde{w}_k^{(0)}$  and  $\tilde{\theta}_k^{(1)}$ ,  $\tilde{w}_k^{(1)}$ .

In the sequel, we show the martingale noise driven terms  $\| \mathbb{E}[\tilde{w}_k^{(0)}(\tilde{w}_k^{(0)})^\top ]\|$ ,  $\| \mathbb{E}[\tilde{w}_k^{(0)}(\tilde{\theta}_k^{(0)})^\top ]\|$ ,  $\| \mathbb{E}[\tilde{\theta}_k^{(0)}(\tilde{\theta}_k^{(0)})^\top ]\|$  can be estimated using similar procedures as in Proposition 3-5 from the previous

subsection. Meanwhile the Markovian noise driven terms  $\| \mathbb{E}[\tilde{w}_k^{(1)}(\tilde{w}_k^{(1)})^\top ]\|$  vanish at a faster rate than the former. Throughout this subsection, we set the step sizes to satisfy:

$$
\gamma_ {k} \leq \gamma_ {\infty} ^ {\text {m a r k}} := \gamma_ {\infty} ^ {(0)} \wedge \frac {1 / \sqrt {d _ {\theta} \vee d _ {w}}}{6 p _ {2 2} \mathrm {E} _ {0} ^ {W V}} \wedge \frac {a _ {2 2} / 4}{\widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {0} + \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {3}}, \beta_ {k} \leq \beta_ {\infty} ^ {\text {m a r k}} := \beta_ {\infty} ^ {(0)} \wedge \frac {1}{\sqrt {6 \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {3} ^ {(1 , 1)}}} \wedge \frac {a _ {\Delta}}{8 \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {\theta}}}, \tag {27}
$$

where  $p_{22} = \lambda_{\min}^{-1}(Q_{22})\lambda_{\max}(Q_{22})$ ,  $\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_0$ ,  $\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_3$ ,  $\mathrm{E}_0^{WV}$  are defined in (62), (67), (64), respectively, and  $\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_3^{(1,1)}$ ,  $\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_2^{\widetilde{\theta}}$  are defined in (77), (78), respectively, in the appendix.

Step 1: Bounding  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{w}}$  We first show that the martingale and Markov noise driven iterates converge with different rates as follows:

Lemma 6 Assume A1-2, B1-4 and the step sizes satisfy (27). For any  $k \in \mathbb{N}$ , it holds

$$
\| \mathbb {E} [ \tilde {w} _ {k + 1} ^ {(0)} (\tilde {w} _ {k + 1} ^ {(0)}) ^ {\top} ] \| \leq \prod_ {\ell = 0} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} a _ {2 2}}{2}\right) ^ {2} \frac {\lambda_ {\max } (Q _ {2 2})}{\lambda_ {\min } (Q _ {2 2})} \mathrm {M} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w}} \tag {28}
$$

$$
+ \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {0} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} \prod_ {\ell = j + 1} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} a _ {2 2}}{2}\right) ^ {2} (1 + \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} + \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}}),
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \mathbb {E} [ \tilde {w} _ {k + 1} ^ {(1)} (\tilde {w} _ {k + 1} ^ {(1)}) ^ {\top} ] \| \leq \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {1} \prod_ {\ell = 0} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} a _ {2 2}}{2}\right) ^ {2} + \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {2} \gamma_ {k} ^ {2} \left(\mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {w}}\right) + \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {4} \gamma_ {k} ^ {2} \tag {29} \\ + \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {3} \gamma_ {k + 1} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} \prod_ {\ell = j + 1} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} a _ {2 2}}{2}\right) ^ {2} (\mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}}), \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_0,\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_1,\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_2,\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_3,\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_4$  are constants defined in (62), (67) in the appendix.

Let us compare the 'steady-state' error on the right hand side of both inequalities: second term of (28) and the second to fourth term of (29). We observe those in the Markovian noise driven iterates  $\tilde{w}_k^{(1)}$  are  $\mathcal{O}(\gamma_k)$  times smaller than the martingale noise driven counterparts, indicating a faster convergence. This is roughly due to the special structure of the Markovian noise in  $V_{k}^{(1)},W_{k}^{(1)}$ , where each term can be written as successive differences of a bounded sequence, e.g.,  $V_{k}^{(1)}\approx \xi_{k} - \xi_{k + 1}$ . When the linear SA (26) is run over a long time horizon, the noise terms from consecutive iterations (roughly) cancel each other, leading to a significantly a smaller 'steady-state' error.

Using  $\tilde{w}_k = \tilde{w}_k^{(0)} + \tilde{w}_k^{(1)}$  together with the above lemma give the following estimate for  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{w}}$ :

Proposition 7 Assume A1-2, B1-4 and the step sizes satisfy (27). For any  $k \in \mathbb{N}$ , it holds

$$
\mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {w}} \leq \prod_ {\ell = 0} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} a _ {2 2}}{4}\right) \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w}} + \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {w}} \gamma_ {k + 1} + \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {w}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} \prod_ {\ell = j + 1} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} a _ {2 2}}{4}\right) \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {3} ^ {\tilde {w}} \gamma_ {k} ^ {2} \mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}},
$$

where  $\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_0^{\tilde{w}},\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_1^{\tilde{w}},\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_2^{\tilde{w}},\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_3^{\tilde{w}}$  are defined in (67) in the appendix.

We note in passing that by considering a special case with  $\mathrm{M}_k^\bar{\theta} = 0$  for all  $k$ , the above proposition generalizes (Srikant and Ying, 2019, Theorem 7) for linear one timescale SA with Markovian noise.

In a similar vein to the proof of Theorem 1, we bound the cross term  $\| \mathbb{E}[\tilde{\theta}_k^{(0)}(\tilde{w}_k^{(0)})^\top ]\|$  as:

Lemma 8 Assume A1-2, B1-4 and the step sizes satisfy (27). For any  $k \in \mathbb{N}$ , it holds

$$
\| \mathbb {E} [ \tilde {\theta} _ {k + 1} ^ {(0)} (\tilde {w} _ {k + 1} ^ {(0)}) ^ {\top} ] \| \leq \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} \prod_ {\ell = 0} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} a _ {2 2}}{4}\right) + \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} \beta_ {k + 1} + \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} \prod_ {\ell = j + 1} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\gamma_ {\ell} a _ {2 2}}{4}\right) \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}},
$$

where the constants  $\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_0^{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{w}},\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_1^{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{w}},\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_2^{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{w}}$  are defined in (73) in the appendix.

However, we observe that it is unnecessary to derive a similar (tight) bound for  $\| \mathbb{E}[\tilde{\theta}_k^{(1)}(\tilde{w}_k^{(1)})^\top ]\|$  as in the above lemma. The reason is that as observed in Lemma 6, the Markovian noise driven terms are anticipated to be sufficiently small compared to the martingale noise driven terms. In particular, a crude bound suffices to obtain the desirable convergence rate of  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta}}$ , as we observe next.

Step 2: Bounding  $\mathbf{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta}}$  Again we consider the bounds on  $\| \mathbb{E}[\tilde{\theta}_{k + 1}^{(0)}(\tilde{\theta}_{k + 1}^{(0)})^\top ]\|$  and  $\mathbb{E}[\| \tilde{\theta}_{k + 1}^{(1)}\| ^2 ]$  separately. As we show in the appendix, both bounds are comparable as the Markovian noise term admits a successive difference structure. Using the decomposition  $\tilde{\theta}_k = \tilde{\theta}_k^{(0)} + \tilde{\theta}_k^{(1)}$ , we obtain:

Proposition 9 Assume A1-2, B1-4 and the step sizes satisfy (27). For any  $k \in \mathbb{N}$ , it holds

$$
\mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \leq \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \prod_ {\ell = 0} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\beta_ {\ell} a _ {\Delta}}{4}\right) + \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \beta_ {k + 1} + \widetilde {\mathrm {C}} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {i} ^ {2} \prod_ {\ell = j + 1} ^ {k} \left(1 - \frac {\beta_ {\ell} a _ {\Delta}}{4}\right) \mathrm {M} _ {i} ^ {\tilde {\theta}}, \tag {30}
$$

where the constants  $\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_0^\tilde{\theta}$ ,  $\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_1^\tilde{\theta}$ ,  $\widetilde{\mathrm{C}}_2^\tilde{\theta}$  are defined in (78) in the appendix.

Equipped with Proposition 9, we can repeat the same steps as in (24) to derive an upper bound for  $\mathrm{M}_k^\theta$  through solving the recursive inequality (30). Similar steps also apply for yielding (15).

# 4. Tightness of the Finite-time Error Bounds

This section examines the tightness of our finite time error bounds in Theorem 1, 2 through characterizing the squared error  $\mathbb{E}[\| \theta_k - \theta^\star \| ^2 ]$  with expansion. We consider the assumption:

A5 There exist matrices  $\Sigma^{11},\Sigma^{12},\Sigma^{22}$ , and a constant  $m_{VW}^{\exp}\geq 0$  such that for all  $j\in \mathbb{N}$ , it holds  $\| \mathbb{E}[V_jV_j^\top ] - \Sigma^{11}\| \vee \| \mathbb{E}[W_jW_j^\top ] - \Sigma^{22}\| \vee \| \mathbb{E}[V_jW_j^\top ] - \Sigma^{12}\| \leq m_{VW}^{\exp}(\| \mathbb{E}[\theta_k\theta_k^\top ]\| +\| [w_kw_k^\top ]\|)$ .

Note that A5 implies A4 and therefore poses a stronger assumption. We have

Theorem 10 Assume A1-3, A5 and for all  $k \in \mathbb{N}$ , we have  $\gamma_k \in [0, \gamma_\infty^{\mathrm{mtg}}]$ ,  $\beta_k \in [0, \beta_\infty^{\mathrm{exp}}]$  and  $\kappa \in [0, \kappa_\infty^{\mathrm{exp}}]$ , where  $\gamma_\infty^{\mathrm{mtg}}, \beta_\infty^{\mathrm{exp}}, \kappa_\infty^{\mathrm{exp}}$  are constants defined in (20), (83), (82) in the appendix. Then for any  $k \geq k_0^{\mathrm{exp}} \coloneqq \min\{\ell : \sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1} \beta_j \geq \log(2)/(2\|\Delta\|)\}$ , the following expansion holds

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \| \theta_ {k} - \theta^ {\star} \| ^ {2} \right] = I _ {k} + J _ {k}. \tag {31}
$$

The leading term  $I_{k}$  is given by the following explicit formula

$$
I _ {k} := \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} ^ {2} \mathrm {T r} \left(\prod_ {\ell = j + 1} ^ {k} (\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {\ell} \Delta) \Sigma \left\{\prod_ {\ell = j + 1} ^ {k} (\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {\ell} \Delta) \right\} ^ {\top}\right),
$$

where  $\Sigma := \Sigma^{11} + A_{12}A_{22}^{-1}\Sigma^{22}A_{22}^{-\top}A_{12}^{\top} + \Sigma^{12}A_{22}^{-\top}A_{12}^{\top} + A_{12}A_{22}^{-1}\Sigma^{21}$ . Meanwhile, the following two-sided inequality holds

$$
\mathrm {C} _ {3} ^ {\exp} \operatorname {T r} (\Sigma) \leq \frac {I _ {k}}{\beta_ {k}} \leq \mathrm {C} _ {4} ^ {\exp} \operatorname {T r} (\Sigma), \tag {32}
$$

and  $J_{k}$  is bounded by

$$
\left| J _ {k} \right| \leq \mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\exp} \prod_ {\ell = 0} ^ {k - 1} \left(1 - \frac {a _ {\Delta}}{4} \beta_ {\ell}\right) \mathrm {V} _ {0} + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\exp} \beta_ {k} \left(\gamma_ {k} + \frac {\beta_ {k}}{\gamma_ {k}}\right), \tag {33}
$$

where  $\mathrm{V}_0$  was defined in (11). All constants  $\mathrm{C}_0^{\mathrm{exp}}$ ,  $\mathrm{C}_1^{\mathrm{exp}}$ ,  $\mathrm{C}_3^{\mathrm{exp}}$ ,  $\mathrm{C}_4^{\mathrm{exp}}$  are given in (109), (89) and (91) in the appendix, respectively, and they are independent of  $\beta_k$ ,  $\gamma_k$ .

The proof is skipped in the interest of space, and it can be found in Appendix C. Observe that from (33), the dominant term for  $J_{k}$  is given by  $\mathcal{O}\left(\beta_{k}\gamma_{k} + \frac{\beta_{k}^{2}}{\gamma_{k}}\right)$ . As such, using (32), we observe that

$$
| J _ {k} | / I _ {k} = \mathcal {O} \left(\gamma_ {k} + \beta_ {k} / \gamma_ {k}\right)
$$

If  $\lim_{k\to \infty}\beta_k / \gamma_k = 0$ , we have  $\lim_{k\to \infty}|J_k| / I_k = 0$ . Combining (31), (32) shows that the expected error  $\mathbb{E}[\|\theta_k - \theta^\star\|^2]$  is lower bounded by  $\Omega(\beta_k)$ .

We note that the assumptions A1-3, A5 imposed by the theorem imply A1-A4 required by Theorem 1. Hence, together with (12) in Theorem 1, the above observations constitute a matching lower bound on the convergence rate of linear two timescale SA with martingale noise. For the Markovian noise setting, we observe that if we impose the assumption that the random elements  $(X_{k})_{k\geq 0}$  are i.i.d., and  $\widetilde{b_i} (x),\widetilde{A}_{ij}(x)$  are bounded above for any  $i,j = 1,2$  and  $x\in \mathsf{X}$ , then A5, B2-B3 can be satisfied. Therefore, the lower bound on the convergence rate also holds.

# 5. Numerical Experiments, Conclusions

We present numerical experiments to support our theoretical claims. We consider (a) a toy example with a randomly generated problem parameters  $b_{i}, A_{ij}$  and i.i.d. samples  $(X_{k})_{k\in \mathbb{N}}$  such that  $\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{b}_i(X_k)] = b_i$ ,  $\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{A}_{ij}(X_k)] = A_{ij}$ , (b) the Garnet problem (Geist and Scherrer, 2014) with the GTD algorithm (Sutton et al., 2009a) using  $X_{k}$  from a simulated Markov chain. For example (a), we compute the stationary point  $\theta^{\star}$ ,  $w^{\star}$  exactly using (7); for example (b), while it is known that  $w^{\star} = 0$ , the solution  $\theta^{\star}$  is computed using Monte Carlo simulation of the matrices  $\widetilde{b}_i(X_k), \widetilde{A}_{ij}(X_k)$  with  $2 \cdot 10^{9}$  iterations. The step sizes are chosen as  $\beta_{k} = c^{\beta} / (k_{0}^{\beta} + k)$ ,  $\gamma_{k} = c^{\gamma} / (k_{0}^{\beta} + k)^{\sigma}$  with  $\sigma \in \{0.5, 0.67, 0.75\}$ . In the toy example (a), we have  $d_{\theta} = d_{w} = 10$ ,  $k_0^\beta = 10^4$ ,  $k_0^\gamma = 10^7$ ,  $c^{\beta} = 140$ ,  $c^{\gamma} = 300$ ; while for the Garnet problem (b), we have  $k_0^\beta = 8 \cdot 10^5$ ,  $k_0^\gamma = 2 \cdot 10^5$ ,  $c^{\beta} = 2300$ ,  $c^{\gamma} = 120$ . Garnet problem is generated from family  $n_S = 30$ ,  $n_A = 2$ ,  $b = 2$ ,  $p = 8$ , see (Geist and Scherrer, 2014). Further details about both experiments are described in Appendix E.

![](images/b9ea507d97990c6995305fa7f9b21847709e19121858d70e391acb65700616b3.jpg)  
Figure 1: Deviations from stationary point  $(\theta^{\star}, w^{\star})$  normalized by step sizes  $\beta_{k}, \gamma_{k}$ : (a,b) the toy example, note we also show  $I_{k}$  using the exact formula in Theorem 10 (unnormized plot also available in the Appendix); (c,d) the Garnet problem.

![](images/f12b0bf02059ad4172fec08018e51ce61a3dcbf741b60a74a61bd76e44620dee.jpg)

![](images/1020e9f61a7254082155e683504744626f4571656fbb4f1664cc0447e7ab605b.jpg)

![](images/7b32527e02aeb1b927bb49abb9ba8b8b71672cc3dd4a81be5e056b7d88a7f06a.jpg)

We illustrate the convergence rates of the linear two timescale SA on the two problems in Figure 1. Note that the plots show the (normalized) steady state errors are  $\mathbb{E}[\| \theta_k - \theta^{\star}\| ^2 ] = \mathcal{O}(\beta_k)$ ,  $\mathbb{E}[\| w_k - w^\star \| ^2 ] = \mathcal{O}(\gamma_k)$ , which hold for both examples on martingale and Markovian noise. In addition, they are independent of the choice of  $\sigma$ . These observations agree with our main results.

Conclusions We have provided an improved finite time convergence analysis of the linear two timescale SA on both martingale and Markovian noises with relaxed conditions. Our analysis show

that a tight analysis is possible through deriving and solving a sequence of recursive error bounds.

Future works include the finite time analysis of nonlinear two timescale SA.

# Appendix A. Proof of Observation 1

The following derivation is largely borrowed from (Konda and Tsitsiklis, 2004) and is repeated here for completeness. We begin by substituting  $\tilde{\theta}_k$  into (3) to obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \tilde {\theta} _ {k + 1} = \left(\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} A _ {1 1}\right) \theta_ {k} - \beta_ {k} A _ {1 2} w _ {k} - \theta^ {\star} + \beta_ {k} b _ {1} - \beta_ {k} V _ {k + 1} \\ = \left(\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} A _ {1 1}\right) \tilde {\theta} _ {k} - \beta_ {k} A _ {1 1} \theta^ {\star} - \beta_ {k} A _ {1 2} \left(\tilde {w} _ {k} + w ^ {\star} - C _ {k - 1} \tilde {\theta} _ {k}\right) + \beta_ {k} b _ {1} - \beta_ {k} V _ {k + 1} \\ = (\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} (A _ {1 1} - A _ {1 2} A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} A _ {2 1} - A _ {1 2} L _ {k})) \tilde {\theta} _ {k} - \beta_ {k} A _ {1 2} \tilde {w} _ {k} - \beta_ {k} (A _ {1 2} w ^ {\star} + A _ {1 1} \theta^ {\star} - b _ {1}) - \beta_ {k} V _ {k + 1}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Notice that

$$
A _ {1 2} w ^ {\star} + A _ {1 1} \theta^ {\star} - b _ {1} = (A _ {1 1} - A _ {1 2} A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} A _ {2 1}) \theta^ {\star} + A _ {1 2} A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} b _ {2} - b _ {1} = 0.
$$

The above yields

$$
\tilde {\theta} _ {k + 1} = \left(\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k}\right) \tilde {\theta} _ {k} - \beta_ {k} A _ {1 2} \tilde {w} _ {k} - \beta_ {k} V _ {k + 1}.
$$

Next, we observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} w _ {k + 1} - w ^ {\star} = \left(\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 2}\right) w _ {k} - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 1} \theta_ {k} - w ^ {\star} + \gamma_ {k} b _ {2} - \gamma_ {k} W _ {k + 1} \\ = \left(\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 2}\right) \left(w _ {k} - w ^ {\star}\right) - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 2} w ^ {\star} - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 1} \theta_ {k} + \gamma_ {k} b _ {2} - \gamma_ {k} W _ {k + 1} \\ = \left(\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 2}\right) \left(w _ {k} - w ^ {\star}\right) - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 1} \left(\theta_ {k} - \theta^ {\star}\right) - \gamma_ {k} W _ {k + 1} \\ \end{array}
$$

Substitute  $\tilde{w}_k$  into (4) and using (18) yield:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \tilde {w} _ {k + 1} = \left(\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 2}\right) \left(w _ {k} - w ^ {\star}\right) - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 1} \tilde {\theta} _ {k} + C _ {k} \tilde {\theta} _ {k + 1} - \gamma_ {k} W _ {k + 1} \\ = \left(\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 2}\right) \tilde {w} _ {k} - \left(\left(\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 2}\right) C _ {k - 1} + \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 1}\right) \tilde {\theta} _ {k} + C _ {k} \left(\left(\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k}\right) \tilde {\theta} _ {k} - \beta_ {k} A _ {1 2} \tilde {w} _ {k}\right) \\ - \beta_ {k} C _ {k} V _ {k + 1} - \gamma_ {k} W _ {k + 1} \\ = \left(\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} B _ {2 2} ^ {k}\right) \tilde {w} _ {k} - \left(C _ {k - 1} - \gamma_ {k} \left(A _ {2 2} C _ {k - 1} - A _ {2 1}\right) - C _ {k} \left(\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k}\right)\right) \tilde {\theta} _ {k} - \beta_ {k} C _ {k} V _ {k + 1} - \gamma_ {k} W _ {k + 1} \\ \end{array}
$$

We observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} C _ {k - 1} - \gamma_ {k} \left(A _ {2 2} C _ {k - 1} - A _ {2 1}\right) - C _ {k} \left(\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k}\right) \\ = L _ {k} + A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} A _ {2 1} - \left(L _ {k + 1} + A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} A _ {2 1}\right) \left(\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k}\right) - \gamma_ {k} \left(A _ {2 2} C _ {k - 1} - A _ {2 1}\right) \\ = L _ {k} - \left(L _ {k} - \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 2} L _ {k} + \beta_ {k} A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} A _ {2 1} B _ {1 1} ^ {k}\right) - \beta_ {k} A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} A _ {2 1} B _ {1 1} ^ {k} - \gamma_ {k} \left(A _ {2 2} C _ {k - 1} - A _ {2 1}\right) \\ = \gamma_ {k} A _ {2 2} L _ {k} - \gamma_ {k} \left(A _ {2 2} \left(L _ {k} + A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} A _ {2 1}\right) - A _ {2 1}\right) = 0. \\ \end{array}
$$

The above yields

$$
\tilde {w} _ {k + 1} = (\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} B _ {2 2} ^ {k}) \tilde {w} _ {k} - \beta_ {k} C _ {k} V _ {k + 1} - \gamma_ {k} W _ {k + 1}.
$$

# Appendix B. Detailed Proofs for Section 3

Before we proceed to proving the main results of Section 3, we first study a few properties of the two timescale linear SA scheme.

To facilitate our discussions next, we define the constant:

$$
\mathrm {C} _ {\infty} := \sqrt {\lambda_ {\min} (Q _ {\Delta}) ^ {- 1} \lambda_ {\max} (Q _ {2 2})} \mathrm {L} _ {\infty} + \| A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} A _ {2 1} \|,
$$

where  $\| C_k\| \leq \mathrm{C}_{\infty}$  for any  $k\geq 0$  . Then, as we have  $\theta_{k}\theta_{k}^{\top}\preceq 2\tilde{\theta}_{k}\tilde{\theta}_{k}^{\top} + 2\theta^{\star}(\theta^{\star})^{\top}$  , it holds

$$
\| \mathbb {E} [ \theta_ {k} \theta_ {k} ^ {\top} ] \| \leq 2 \big \{\mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \| \theta^ {\star} (\theta^ {\star}) ^ {\top} \| \big \}, \quad \| \mathbb {E} [ w _ {k} w _ {k} ^ {\top} ] \| \leq 3 \big \{\mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {w}} + \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \mathrm {C} _ {\infty} ^ {2} + \| w ^ {\star} (w ^ {\star}) ^ {\top} \| \big \}
$$

The noise terms  $V_{k}$ ,  $W_{k}$  can then be estimated in terms of the transformed variables  $\tilde{\theta}_{k}$ ,  $\tilde{w}_{k}$  and their variances  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta}}$ ,  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{w}}$ . In particular, combining with A4 yields

$$
\left\| \mathbb {E} \left[ V _ {k + 1} V _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} \right] \right\| \leq \tilde {m} _ {V} \left(1 + \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {w}}\right), \quad \left\| \mathbb {E} \left[ W _ {k + 1} W _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} \right] \right\| \leq \tilde {m} _ {W} \left(1 + \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {w}}\right) \tag {34}
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbb {E} \left[ V _ {k + 1} W _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} \right] \right\| \leq \tilde {m} _ {V W} \left(1 + \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {w}}\right) \tag {35}
$$

where

$$
\frac {\tilde {m} _ {V}}{m _ {V}} = \frac {\tilde {m} _ {W}}{m _ {W}} = (1 + 2 \| \theta^ {\star} (\theta^ {\star}) ^ {\top} \| + 3 \| w ^ {\star} (w ^ {\star}) ^ {\top} \|) \vee (2 + 3 \mathrm {C} _ {\infty} ^ {2}) \vee 3 \tag {36}
$$

$$
\tilde {m} _ {V W} = \frac {\sqrt {d _ {\theta} d _ {w}}}{2} (\tilde {m} _ {W} + \tilde {m} _ {V})
$$

We also define a few constants related to the matrices  $Q_{\Delta}, Q_{22}$  associated with the Hurwitz matrices  $\Delta, A_{22}$  in (8). Set  $p_{\Delta} := \lambda_{\min}^{-1}(Q_{\Delta})\lambda_{\max}(Q_{\Delta})$ ,  $p_{22} := \lambda_{\min}^{-1}(Q_{22})\lambda_{\max}(Q_{22})$ ,  $p_{22,\Delta} := \sqrt{p_{22}p_{\Delta}}$ . Moreover, for any  $a > 0$ , we set

$$
\varrho^ {a} := \frac {2}{a} \varsigma \max  \{1, a _ {2 2} / (4 a _ {\Delta}) \} \vee \frac {4}{a} (\varsigma) ^ {3}.
$$

Next, we study the contraction properties of  $\mathrm{I} - \beta_{k}B_{11}^{k}$  and  $\mathrm{I} - \gamma_{k}B_{22}^{k}$  that appear in the transformed two timescale SA (18),(19). Using (9), we observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k} \right\| _ {Q _ {\Delta}} = \left\| \mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} \Delta + \beta_ {k} A _ {1 2} L _ {k} \right\| _ {Q _ {\Delta}} \leq \left\| \mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} \Delta \right\| _ {Q _ {\Delta}} + \beta_ {k} \left\| A _ {1 2} \right\| _ {Q _ {2 2}, Q _ {\Delta}} \left\| L _ {k} \right\| _ {Q _ {\Delta}, Q _ {2 2}} \\ \leq \left(1 - \beta_ {k} a _ {\Delta}\right) + \beta_ {k} \| A _ {1 2} \| _ {Q _ {2 2}, Q _ {\Delta}} \| L _ {k} \| _ {Q _ {\Delta}, Q _ {2 2}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Recalling that  $\| L_k\|_{Q_{\Delta},Q_{22}}\leq \mathrm{L}_{\infty}$ , the above inequality yields

$$
\left\| \mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k} \right\| _ {Q _ {\Delta}} \leq 1 - (1 / 2) \beta_ {k} a _ {\Delta}. \tag {37}
$$

Since  $\| \mathrm{I} - \gamma_k B_{22}^k \|_{Q_{22}} \leq \| \mathrm{I} - \gamma_k A_{22} \|_{Q_{22}} + \beta_k \| C_k A_{12} \|_{Q_{22}}$ , we obtain the contraction:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| I - \gamma_ {k} B _ {2 2} ^ {k} \right\| _ {Q _ {2 2}} \leq 1 - \gamma_ {k} a _ {2 2} + \beta_ {k} \left(\mathrm {L} _ {\infty} + \| A _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} A _ {2 1} \| _ {Q _ {\Delta}, Q _ {2 2}}\right) \| A _ {1 2} \| _ {Q _ {2 2}, Q _ {\Delta}} \tag {38} \\ \leq 1 - (1 / 2) \gamma_ {k} a _ {2 2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

The last inequality is due to  $\kappa \leq (a_{22} / 2)\{(L_{\infty} + \| A_{22}^{-1}A_{21}\|_{Q_{\Delta},Q_{22}})\| A_{12}\|_{Q_{22},Q_{\Delta}}\}^{-1}$ . Lastly, the following quantities will be used throughout the analysis:

$$
\Gamma_ {m: n} ^ {(1)} := \prod_ {i = m} ^ {n} (\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {i} B _ {1 1} ^ {i}), \quad \Gamma_ {m: n} ^ {(2)} := \prod_ {i = m} ^ {n} (\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {i} B _ {2 2} ^ {i}),
$$

$$
G _ {m: n} ^ {(1)} := \prod_ {i = m} ^ {n} \left(1 - (1 / 2) \beta_ {i} a _ {\Delta}\right), \quad G _ {m: n} ^ {(2)} := \prod_ {i = m} ^ {n} \left(1 - (1 / 2) \gamma_ {i} a _ {2 2}\right).
$$

As a convention, we define  $\Gamma_{m:n}^{(1)} = \Gamma_{m:n}^{(2)} = \mathrm{I}$  if  $m > n$ . In particular, for any  $n, m \geq 0$ , we observe the following bound on the operator norm of  $\Gamma_{m:n}^{(1)}$ ,

$$
\| \Gamma_ {m: n} ^ {(1)} \| = \sqrt {p _ {\Delta}} \| \Gamma_ {m: n} ^ {(1)} \| _ {Q _ {\Delta}} \leq \sqrt {p _ {\Delta}} \prod_ {i = m} ^ {n} \| \mathrm {I} - \beta_ {i} B _ {1 1} ^ {i} \| _ {Q _ {\Delta}} \leq \sqrt {p _ {\Delta}} G _ {m: n} ^ {(1)}
$$

Similarly, we have  $\| \Gamma_{m:n}^{(1)}\| \leq \sqrt{p_{22}} G_{m:n}^{(2)}$ . Lastly, we define

$$
\Sigma_ {k} := \mathbb {E} \left[ \tilde {w} _ {k} \tilde {w} _ {k} ^ {\top} \right], \quad \Omega_ {k} := \mathbb {E} \left[ \tilde {\theta} _ {k} \tilde {w} _ {k} ^ {\top} \right], \quad \Theta_ {k} := \mathbb {E} \left[ \tilde {\theta} _ {k} \tilde {\theta} _ {k} ^ {\top} \right],
$$

whose operator norms correspond to  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{w}},\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{w}},\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta}}$  , respectively.

# B.1. Detailed Proof of Theorem 1

This subsection provides proofs to the propositions stated in Section 3.1, as well as providing detailed steps in establishing Theorem 1.

Bounding  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{w}}$  (Proof of Proposition 3) Using (19), as the noise terms are martingale, we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} ^ {\mathcal {F} _ {k}} \left[ \tilde {w} _ {k + 1} \tilde {w} _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} \right] = \left(\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} B _ {2 2} ^ {k}\right) \tilde {w} _ {k} \tilde {w} _ {k} ^ {\top} \left(\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} B _ {2 2} ^ {k}\right) ^ {\top} + \gamma_ {k} ^ {2} \mathbb {E} ^ {\mathcal {F} _ {k}} \left[ W _ {k + 1} W _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} \right] \\ + \beta_ {k} ^ {2} C _ {k} \mathbb {E} ^ {\mathcal {F} _ {k}} \left[ V _ {k + 1} V _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} \right] C _ {k} ^ {\top} + \beta_ {k} \gamma_ {k} \left(\mathbb {E} ^ {\mathcal {F} _ {k}} \left[ W _ {k + 1} V _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} \right] C _ {k} ^ {\top} + C _ {k} \mathbb {E} ^ {\mathcal {F} _ {k}} \left[ V _ {k + 1} W _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} \right]\right). \tag {39} \\ \end{array}
$$

Repeatedly applying (39) and taking the total expectation on both sides show

$$
\Sigma_ {k + 1} = \Gamma_ {0: k} ^ {(2)} \Sigma_ {0} \left(\Gamma_ {0: k} ^ {(2)}\right) ^ {\top} + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \Gamma_ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} D _ {j + 1} \left(\Gamma_ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)}\right) ^ {\top}, \tag {40}
$$

where

$$
D _ {k + 1} = \gamma_ {k} ^ {2} \mathbb {E} [ W _ {k + 1} W _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} ] + \beta_ {k} ^ {2} C _ {k} \mathbb {E} [ V _ {k + 1} V _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} ] C _ {k} ^ {\top} + \beta_ {k} \gamma_ {k} \big (\mathbb {E} [ W _ {k + 1} V _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} ] C _ {k} ^ {\top} + C _ {k} \mathbb {E} [ V _ {k + 1} W _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} ] \big).
$$

Using Lemma 23, we observe that

$$
\gamma_ {k} \beta_ {k} \| \mathbb {E} [ W _ {k + 1} V _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} ] C _ {k} ^ {\top} \| \leq \frac {\sqrt {d _ {\theta} d _ {w}}}{2} C _ {\infty} \big (\gamma_ {k} ^ {2} \| \mathbb {E} [ W _ {k + 1} W _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} ] \| + \beta_ {k} ^ {2} \| \mathbb {E} [ V _ {k + 1} V _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} ] \| \big),
$$

Let  $K_{C}:= \max \{C_{\infty}^{2},1\} +\sqrt{d_{\theta}d_{w}} C_{\infty}$  , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| D _ {k + 1} \| \leq \gamma_ {k} ^ {2} \Big (1 + C _ {\infty} \sqrt {d _ {\theta} d _ {w}} \Big) \| \mathbb {E} [ W _ {k + 1} W _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} ] \| + \beta_ {k} ^ {2} C _ {\infty} \Big (C _ {\infty} + \sqrt {d _ {\theta} d _ {w}} \Big) \| \mathbb {E} [ V _ {k + 1} V _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} ] \| \\ \leq K _ {C} \left(\gamma_ {k} ^ {2} \left\{\tilde {m} _ {V} + \tilde {m} _ {V} \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \tilde {m} _ {V} \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {w}} \right\} + \beta_ {k} ^ {2} \left\{\tilde {m} _ {W} + \tilde {m} _ {W} \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \tilde {m} _ {W} \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {w}} \right\}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality is due to (34). Taking the operator norm on both sides of (40) yields

$$
\mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {w}} \leq p _ {2 2} \Big \{\left(G _ {0: k} ^ {(2)}\right) ^ {2} \mathrm {M} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w}} + K _ {C} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \left(G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)}\right) ^ {2} (\gamma_ {j} ^ {2} \tilde {m} _ {V} + \beta_ {j} ^ {2} \tilde {m} _ {W}) \big \{1 + \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} \big \} \Big \}.
$$

Using that  $\beta_{k}\leq \kappa \gamma_{k}$  one writes

$$
\mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {w}} \leq \mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w} ^ {\prime}} \left(G _ {0: k} ^ {(2)}\right) ^ {2} + c _ {0} \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {w} ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} \left(G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)}\right) ^ {2} \left(1 + \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}}\right) + \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {w} ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} \left(G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)}\right) ^ {2} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} \tag {40}
$$

where  $c_{0} = \tilde{m}_{V} + \kappa^{2}\tilde{m}_{W}$ ,  $\mathrm{C}_0^{\tilde{w} '} = p_{22}\mathrm{M}_0^{\tilde{w}}$ ,  $\mathrm{C}_1^{\tilde{w} '} = p_{22}K_C$ , and  $\mathrm{C}_2^{\tilde{w} '} = p_{22}c_0$ . Define:

$$
\tilde {\mathrm {U}} _ {k} = \mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w} ^ {\prime}} \big (G _ {0: k - 1} ^ {(2)} \big) ^ {2} + c _ {0} \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {w} ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k - 1} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} \big (G _ {j + 1: k - 1} ^ {(2)} \big) ^ {2} (1 + \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}}) + \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {w} ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k - 1} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} \big (G _ {j + 1: k - 1} ^ {(2)} \big) ^ {2} \tilde {\mathrm {U}} _ {j},
$$

It is easily seen that the sequence  $(\tilde{\mathrm{U}}_k)_{k\geq 0}$  is given by the following recursion

$$
\tilde {\mathrm {U}} _ {k + 1} = (1 - a _ {2 2} \gamma_ {k} / 2) ^ {2} \tilde {\mathrm {U}} _ {k} + c _ {0} \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {w} ^ {\prime}} \gamma_ {k} ^ {2} (1 + \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {\theta}}) + \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {w} ^ {\prime}} \gamma_ {k} ^ {2} \tilde {\mathrm {U}} _ {k}, \quad \tilde {\mathrm {U}} _ {0} = \mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w} ^ {\prime}}.
$$

Since the step size was chosen such that  $\gamma_k\big(\mathrm{C}_2^{\tilde{w}'} + \big(a_{22}^2 /4\big)\big)\leq \frac{a_{22}}{2}$  [cf. (20)], we have

$$
\tilde {\mathrm {U}} _ {k + 1} \leq (1 - a _ {2 2} \gamma_ {k} / 2) \tilde {\mathrm {U}} _ {k} + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {w} ^ {\prime}} \gamma_ {k} ^ {2} (c _ {0} + c _ {1} \mathrm {M} _ {k} ^ {\tilde {\theta}})
$$

which implies

$$
\tilde {\mathrm {U}} _ {k + 1} \leq \mathrm {C} _ {0} G _ {0: k} ^ {(2)} + c _ {0} \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {w} ^ {\prime}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} (1 + \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}}) G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)}.
$$

Observe that  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{w}}\leq \tilde{\mathrm{U}}_k$ . Applying Corollary 14 shows that  $\sum_{j = 0}^{k}\gamma_{j}^{2}G_{j + 1:k}^{(2)}\leq \varrho^{a_{22} / 2}\gamma_{k + 1}$ , we get

$$
\boxed {\mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {w}} \leq \mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w}} G _ {0: k} ^ {(2)} + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {w}} \gamma_ {k + 1} + \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {w}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}},} \tag {41}
$$

where we recall  $K_{C} \coloneqq \max \{C_{\infty}^{2}, 1\} + \sqrt{d_{\theta}d_{w}} C_{\infty}$ , and

$$
\mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w}} := p _ {2 2} \mathrm {M} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w}}, \quad \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {w}} := p _ {2 2} (\tilde {m} _ {V} + \kappa^ {2} \tilde {m} _ {W}) K _ {C} \varrho^ {a _ {2 2} / 2}, \quad \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {w}} := p _ {2 2} K _ {C} (\tilde {m} _ {V} + \kappa^ {2} \tilde {m} _ {W}). (4 2)
$$

This concludes the proof for Proposition 3.

Bounding  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\bar{\theta},\tilde{w}}$  (Proof of Proposition 4) We proceed by observing the following recursion of  $\Omega_{k}$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \Omega_ {k + 1} = (\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k}) \Omega_ {k} (\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} B _ {2 2} ^ {k}) ^ {\top} - \beta_ {k} A _ {1 2} \Sigma_ {k} (\mathrm {I} - \gamma_ {k} B _ {2 2} ^ {k}) ^ {\top} \\ + \beta_ {k} \gamma_ {k} \mathbb {E} [ V _ {k + 1} W _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} ] + \beta_ {k} ^ {2} \mathbb {E} [ V _ {k + 1} V _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} ] C _ {k} ^ {\top}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Repeatedly applying the recursion gives

$$
\begin{array}{l} \Omega_ {k + 1} = \Gamma_ {0: k} ^ {(1)} \Omega_ {0} \left(\Gamma_ {0: k} ^ {(2)}\right) ^ {\top} - \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} \Gamma_ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} A _ {1 2} \Sigma_ {j} \left(\Gamma_ {j: k} ^ {(2)}\right) ^ {\top} \\ + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} \gamma_ {j} \Gamma_ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} \mathbb {E} \left[ V _ {j + 1} W _ {j + 1} ^ {\top} \right] \left(\Gamma_ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)}\right) ^ {\top} + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} ^ {2} \Gamma_ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} \mathbb {E} \left[ V _ {j + 1} V _ {j + 1} ^ {\top} \right] C _ {j} ^ {\top} \left(\Gamma_ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)}\right) ^ {\top}. \\ \end{array}
$$

The contraction properties (38), (37) result in

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} \leq p _ {2 2, \Delta} \left\{G _ {0: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {0: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} + \| A _ {1 2} \| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} \right\} \tag {43} \\ + p _ {2 2, \Delta} \left\{\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} \gamma_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \| \mathbb {E} [ V _ {j + 1} W _ {j + 1} ^ {\top} ] \| + C _ {\infty} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} ^ {2} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \| \mathbb {E} [ V _ {j + 1} V _ {j + 1} ^ {\top} ] \| \right\} \\ \end{array}
$$

Applying (41), we bound the third last term of (43) as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} \leq \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j: k} ^ {(2)} \big (\mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w}} G _ {0: j - 1} ^ {(2)} + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {w}} \gamma_ {j} + \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {w}} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j - 1} \gamma_ {i} ^ {2} G _ {i + 1: j - 1} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {i} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \big) \\ \leq \frac {2 \mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w}} G _ {0 : k} ^ {(2)}}{a _ {\Delta}} + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {w}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} G _ {j: k} ^ {(2)} \gamma_ {j} + \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {w}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j: k} ^ {(2)} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j - 1} \gamma_ {i} ^ {2} G _ {i + 1: j - 1} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {i} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have used Lemma 12 and  $G_{j+1:k}^{(1)} \leq 1$  in the last inequality. Applying Corollary 14 and Lemma 15, A2 to the second and the last term on the right hand side, respectively, we obtain the following upper bound:

$$
\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} \leq \frac {2 \mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w}} G _ {0 : k} ^ {(2)}}{a _ {\Delta}} + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {w}} \varrho^ {a _ {2 2} / 2} \beta_ {k + 1} + \frac {2 \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {w}}}{a _ {\Delta}} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {i} ^ {2} G _ {i + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {i} ^ {\tilde {\theta}}. \tag {44}
$$

Applying (35), we bound the second last term of (43) as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} \gamma_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \| \mathbb {E} [ V _ {j + 1} W _ {j + 1} ^ {\top} ] \| \leq \tilde {m} _ {V W} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} \gamma_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \big (1 + \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} \big) \\ \leq \tilde {m} _ {V W} \left\{\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} \gamma_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} \gamma_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} \gamma_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} \right\} \tag {45} \\ \leq \tilde {m} _ {V W} \left\{\varrho^ {a _ {2 2} / 2} \beta_ {k + 1} + \kappa \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} \gamma_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} \right\} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality applied Corollary 14 again. We observe

$$
\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} \gamma_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} \leq \frac {\gamma_ {0}}{1 - \gamma_ {0} a _ {2 2} / 2} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k - 1} ^ {(1)} G _ {j: k - 1} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} \tag {46}
$$

Thirdly, we repeat the calculations above and exploit  $\beta_{k} \leq \kappa \gamma_{k}$  to bound

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} ^ {2} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \| \mathbb {E} [ V _ {j + 1} V _ {j + 1} ^ {\top} ] \| \leq \tilde {m} _ {V} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} ^ {2} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \left(1 + \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}}\right) \\ \leq \tilde {m} _ {V} \left\{\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} ^ {2} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} ^ {2} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\varrho}} + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} ^ {2} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} \right\} \tag {47} \\ \leq \tilde {m} _ {V} \Bigl \{\kappa \varrho^ {a _ {2 2} / 2} \beta_ {k + 1} + \kappa^ {2} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \kappa \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} \gamma_ {j} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} \Bigr \}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Combining (44), (45), (46), (47), we conclude that

$$
\boxed {\mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} \leq \mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} G _ {0: k} ^ {(2)} + \mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} \beta_ {k + 1} + \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \gamma_ {j} ^ {2} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(2)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}}} \tag {48}
$$

where

$$
\mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} := p _ {2 2, \Delta} \Big (\mathrm {M} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} + \| A _ {1 2} \| \frac {2 \mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w}}}{a _ {\Delta}} + (\tilde {m} _ {V W} + \kappa C _ {\infty} \tilde {m} _ {V}) \frac {2 \gamma_ {0} \mathrm {C} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {w}}}{a _ {\Delta} (1 - \gamma_ {0} a _ {2 2} / 2)} \Big),
$$

$$
\mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} := p _ {2 2, \Delta} \varrho^ {a _ {2 2} / 2} \Big (\mathrm {C} _ {1} ^ {\tilde {w}} \left(\| A _ {1 2} \| + \frac {\gamma_ {0}}{1 - \gamma_ {0} a _ {2 2} / 2} (\tilde {m} _ {V W} + C _ {\infty} \kappa \tilde {m} _ {V})\right) + \tilde {m} _ {V W} + C _ {\infty} \kappa \tilde {m} _ {V} \Big),
$$

$$
\mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} := p _ {2 2, \Delta} \left(\frac {2 \mathrm {C} _ {2} ^ {\tilde {w}}}{a _ {\Delta}} \left(\| A _ {1 2} \| + \frac {\gamma_ {0}}{1 - \gamma_ {0} a _ {2 2} / 2} \left(\tilde {m} _ {V W} + C _ {\infty} \kappa \tilde {m} _ {V}\right)\right) + \kappa \left(\tilde {m} _ {V W} + C _ {\infty} \kappa \tilde {m} _ {V}\right)\right). \tag {49}
$$

This concludes the proof of Proposition 4.

Bounding  $\mathrm{M}_k^{\tilde{\theta}}$  (Proof of Proposition 5) We observe the following recursion:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} ^ {\mathcal {F} _ {k}} \left[ \tilde {\theta} _ {k + 1} \tilde {\theta} _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} \right] = (\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k}) \mathbb {E} ^ {\mathcal {F} _ {k}} \left[ \tilde {\theta} _ {k} \tilde {\theta} _ {k} ^ {\top} \right] (\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k}) ^ {\top} + \beta_ {k} ^ {2} A _ {1 2} \mathbb {E} ^ {\mathcal {F} _ {k}} \left[ \tilde {w} _ {k} \tilde {w} _ {k} ^ {\top} \right] A _ {1 2} ^ {\top} \\ + \beta_ {k} ^ {2} \mathbb {E} ^ {\mathcal {F} _ {k}} \left[ V _ {k + 1} V _ {k + 1} ^ {\top} \right] - \beta_ {k} \left(\left(\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k}\right) \mathbb {E} ^ {\mathcal {F} _ {k}} \left[ \tilde {\theta} _ {k} \tilde {w} _ {k} ^ {\top} \right] A _ {1 2} ^ {\top} + A _ {1 2} \mathbb {E} ^ {\mathcal {F} _ {k}} \left[ \tilde {w} _ {k} \tilde {\theta} _ {k} ^ {\top} \right] \left(\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {k} B _ {1 1} ^ {k}\right) ^ {\top}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

Taking total expectations and evaluating the recursion gives

$$
\begin{array}{l} \Theta_ {k + 1} = \Gamma_ {0: k} ^ {(1)} \Theta_ {0} (\Gamma_ {0: k} ^ {(1)}) ^ {\top} + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} ^ {2} \Gamma_ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} (A _ {1 2} \Sigma_ {j} A _ {1 2} ^ {\top} + \mathbb {E} [ V _ {j + 1} V _ {j + 1} ^ {\top} ]) (\Gamma_ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)}) ^ {\top} \\ - \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} \Gamma_ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} ((\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {j} B _ {1 1} ^ {j}) \Omega_ {j} A _ {1 2} ^ {\top} + A _ {1 2} \Omega_ {j} ^ {\top} (\mathrm {I} - \beta_ {j} B _ {1 1} ^ {j}) ^ {\top}) \left(\Gamma_ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)}\right) ^ {\top} \\ \end{array}
$$

The above implies

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \leq p _ {\Delta} \left\{\left(G _ {0: k} ^ {(1)}\right) ^ {2} \mathrm {M} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + 2 \| A _ {1 2} \| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} \left(G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)}\right) ^ {2} \left(1 - \beta_ {j} a _ {\Delta} / 2\right) \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} \right\} \tag {50} \\ + p _ {\Delta} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} ^ {2} (G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)}) ^ {2} \big (\| A _ {1 2} \| ^ {2} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}} + \| \mathbb {E} [ V _ {j + 1} V _ {j + 1} ^ {\top} ] \| \big) \\ \end{array}
$$

Applying (34) and Corollary 14 yield

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {M} _ {k + 1} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \leq p _ {\Delta} \left\{\left(G _ {0: k} ^ {(1)}\right) ^ {2} \mathrm {M} _ {0} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} + \tilde {m} _ {V} \varrho^ {a _ {\Delta} / 2} \beta_ {k + 1} + \tilde {m} _ {V} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} ^ {2} \left(G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)}\right) ^ {2} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}} \right\} \tag {51} \\ + 2 p _ {\Delta} \| A _ {1 2} \| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} G _ {j: k} ^ {(1)} G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {\theta}, \tilde {w}} + p _ {\Delta} \big (\| A _ {1 2} \| ^ {2} + \tilde {m} _ {V} \big) \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {k} \beta_ {j} ^ {2} (G _ {j + 1: k} ^ {(1)}) ^ {2} \mathrm {M} _ {j} ^ {\tilde {w}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

Applying (48), we can bound the second last term in (51) as

$$
